	**************************************
	security profiles
		packet flow with certificate
			the deep ssl inspection mechanism 
				designed based on a controlled man-in-the-middle (mitm) attack

				the client wants to connect to a secure site (google.com])
				the https traffic is encrypted from the origin
				the firewall cannot see inside the encrypted traffic 
					**because it does not have google's private key, so it has to play the role of a man in the middle

			how ssl inspection works exactly 
				plaintext[ client ] <--- (1) fortigate ca ---> [ fortigate ] <--- (2) real server cert ---> [ internet server ]

				phase 1
					fortigate communicates with the outside world (destination server side)
						1. the client sends a connection request to google.com

						2. fortigate holds the request and independently establishes an ssl connection with the google server
						
						3. the google server gives the fortigate its real certificate (issued by a root ca such as digicert or let's encrypt)
						
						4. fortigate validates the google certificate using its own list of trusted root cas to ensure that it is the original site

				phase 2
					decrypt, inspect, and package (client side)
						1. fortigate decrypts the data received from google and checks for ips/antivirus signatures
						
						2. now fortigate must re-encrypt this data and deliver it to the client
							but there is a problem
								fortigate cannot sign the real google certificate (because it does not have google's private key)
						
						3. this is where the fortigate ca comes into play 
							on-the-fly
								fortigate generates a fake but fully valid certificate called google.com
								signs it with fortinet_ca_ssl (or the organization's own ca) and sends it to the client

				phase 3
					why doesn't the client system give an error?

					if the fortinet_ca_ssl public key is not already installed on the client browser/system
					the browser gives a severe security error warning (untrusted certificate / mitm warning)

					however, if this ca is installed in the trusted root certification authorities section of the client system (via active directory / gpo)
					the browser accepts the fake fortigate certificate, the green https lock is displayed, and the connection is established without problems

				logic correction section
					client to fortigate traffic the client's initial traffic is encrypted with a fake fortigate-made certificate (signed by fortica) rather than the client encrypting itself with fortica
					
					fortigate to internet traffic fortigate traffic to the internet is encrypted with the same site's real certificate (google's public key)
					root cas inside the fortigate are only used for validation of external site certificates, not for signing

				imagine a client wants to send an email to an external server with a locked mailbox
					1. fortigate intercepts the email
					
					2. with its own key (fortica), it opens the client's mailbox, reads the email (virus and content inspection)
					
					3. if the content is intact, it places the email in a new mailbox, locks it with the destination server's (google's) public key, and sends it out to the internet
					
					4. on the way back, the same process is repeated in reverse

			in fortigate terminology, ssl inspection and deep inspection are essentially the same concept
				but there is a slight difference between them in terms of naming structure in menus and technical concepts
					1. key concept (naming difference)
						ssl inspection (general concept)
							this is the general name for the mechanism for inspecting encrypted traffic in the firewall. this process has two methods or modes
								full ssl inspection (which fortigate calls deep inspection)

								ssl certificate inspection

						deep inspection (advanced mode)
							this is the name that fortigate refers to exactly the method of opening the packet (decryption)
								in this mode, the firewall opens the content inside the https traffic, examines the data body (payload) and re-encrypts it

					2. comparing the two ssl inspection modes in fortigate
						in the fortigate settings (in the security profiles > ssl/ssh inspection section), you have two main choices
							certificate inspection  
								ssl/tls header only (sni and certificate section) 
								content view does not see 
								need install ca certificate ? no (no browser error)
								cpu usage low
								ability to block vpns (like psiphon) weak (based on domain/sni only)

							deep inspection (full ssl inspection)
								full traffic decryption (full decryption & inspection)
								the content inside the traffic sees all the content inside the packet (urls, files, texts)
								requires ca on the client (fortigate ca must be installed)
								cpu impact very high (due to heavy decrypt/encrypt processing)
								ability to block vpns (like psiphon) very strong (identifies unauthorized traffic inside tls)



					**certificate inspection
						it's like reading the envelope (address and recipient name) but not opening the inside of the letter

					**deep inspection (or deep ssl inspection)
						it's like opening the envelope completely, reading the text inside, scanning for viruses, and then repacking it in a new envelope and sending it

			how does psiphon try to connect?
				psiphon knows that the firewall has blocked unknown ports
				it tries to disguise its traffic as regular https traffic (port 443) to get through the firewall

				plaintext [ client (psiphon) ] === ( fake https tunnel ) ===> [ fortigate ] ===> [ psiphon server ]

				step 1: initial ssl handshake
					1. psiphon software sends an ssl/tls request to one of its dedicated servers on the internet

					2. to avoid detection, it writes the name of a very authoritative site 
						cdn.cloudflare.com or aws.amazon.com
						in the sni (server name indication) field 
						this technique is called domain fronting

					3. in normal mode (without deep inspection), the firewall only sees appearances, thinks the user is opening the cloudflare site and allows it to pass

				how does fortigate block psiphon with deep inspection?
					when deep inspection is enabled
						fortigate plays the exact same man-in-the-middle (mitm) role and disrupts the psiphon connection process
							plaintext [ client (psiphon) ] <--- (1) fortigate ca ---> [ fortigate ] <--- (2) real ssl ---> [ psiphon server ]|[ 3. inspection & block ]

						1. breaking the encrypted tunnel (decryption)
							client request
								psiphon sends the ssl request

							fortigate response
								fortigate disconnects, connects to the destination server itself, generates a fake certificate with the fortigate ca and sends it to the client (psiphon)

							**key point
								unlike browsers, psiphon has a strict list of authorized cas (certificate pinning)
								when it sees that the certificate is signed by the fortigate ca, it knows that someone is eavesdropping (inspection) and immediately terminates the https connection

						2. if psiphon tries to send non-ssl traffic (protocol validation)
							if psiphon tries to pass ssh traffic or its own private tunnel directly over port 443 (without the tls standard)
								since fortigate has opened the packet (decrypt), it finds that the data structure inside the packet does not comply with the tls protocol rfc standards

								fortigate gives an error and drops the packet (invalid ssl packet)

						3. checking the content inside the packet (application control + pattern matching)
							if for any reason the ssl handshake is performed and the traffic is opened
								the fortigate application control engine scans the opened content (payload)

								psiphon signature patterns and behaviors (such as requesting a list of servers or special psiphon header structures) are detected by fortiguard signatures and the firewall blocks the packets

				full comparison table (suitable for classroom presentation)
					normal https traffic (google) 
						standard tls traffic to port 443
						certificate generation is performed by fortigate (by fortigate ca)
						client-side software response the browser accepts the certificate (because it is trusted)
						result in deep inspection, the traffic is inspected and passes

					psiphon traffic
						manipulated tls or masked ssh to 443
						certificate generation is performed by fortigate (by fortigate ca)
						psiphon rejects the certificate (because it has a pinning mechanism) or reports a mismatch
						the encryption tunnel is broken, the pattern is exposed and blocked

				the main reason psiphon fails against deep inspection	
					psiphon works on the basis of zero trust / certificate pinning
					when you enable deep inspection, fortigate puts its own certificate in the middle
					psiphon notices this certificate change (mitm)
					as a result, either psiphon itself drops the connection to stay safe, or fortigate blocks it because the traffic in the packet is non-standard

			client hello message in the tls/ssl protocol and then the changes that tools like psiphon make to it to bypass filtering	
				1. standard tls client hello message structure
					when the browser wants to establish a secure https connection, the first message it sends to the server is the client hello

					this message contains the following parts
						plaintext
						+---------------------------------------------------------+
						| record header (content type: handshake 0x22) |
						+----------------------------------------------------+
						| handshake header (type: client hello 0x01) |
						+-----------------------------------------------------+
						| client version (e.g., tls 1.2 / tls 1.3) |
						+-----------------------------------------------------+
						| random bytes (32 bytes for session / anti-reply) |
						+----------------------------------------------------+
						| session id |
						+-----------------------------------------------------+
						| cipher suites (list of supported encryption algorithms)|
						+----------------------------------------------------+
						| compression methods |
						+------------------------------------------------------+
						| extensions (main part to identify and modify): |
						| - extension: server name indication (sni) |
						| - extension: supported groups (elliptic curves) |
						| - extension: application-layer protocol negotiation |
						| - extension: key share (tls 1.3) |
						+-----------------------------------------------------+

				2. which parts of tls exactly does psiphon modify?
					to bypass firewalls and remain undetected, psiphon mainly manipulates three main parts of this structure
						a) manipulation of the extension: server name indication (sni) section
							psiphon modification (domain fronting)
								psiphon places the name of a fully valid and trusted domain such as cdn.cloudflare.com or ajax.googleapis.com in the sni section (which specifies the destination domain name) while in the main body of the http request, which is inside the encrypted frame, the real destination is something else

							fortigate detection
								in deep inspection mode, fortigate, after decrypting the traffic, compares the domain name in the sni with the domain name in the http host header
								if there is a mismatch (sni mismatch), it immediately blocks the connection

						b) cipher suites and ja3 fingerprint manipulation
							each browser or operating system sends a specific and fixed list of encryption algorithms (cipher suites) and extensions, which is called the ja3 fingerprint

							psiphon modification
								psiphon may change the order or list of encryption algorithms in cipher suites to go into obfuscated mode or resemble other programs, or use custom clients (other than standard browsers such as chrome or firefox)

							fortigate detection
								fortigate and ips systems analyze the ja3/ja4 pattern of this message
								if the cipher suites pattern does not match the common browsers or the proxy software fingerprint, it is detected

						c) use of tls-alpn (application-layer protocol negotiation)
							psiphon modification
								psiphon may try to send unrelated application-layer protocols in the alpn extension to avoid the firewall inspection

							fortigate detection
								fortigate's protocol decoder engine checks whether the protocol negotiated in alpn matches the actual behavior of the application-layer traffic

				3. summary of how deep inspection detects
					how psiphon changes 
						sni extension inserts valid domains exempt from filtering
						cipher suites & order changes fingerprint (ja3/ja4 fingerprint)
						uses its own dedicated servers as server certificate

					how fortigate detects 
						compares sni with real http host header after decryption
						matches handshake pattern with known fortiguard signatures
						rejects fake fortigate certificate by psiphon (certificate pinning) as server certificate

			ja3 and ja4 fingerprints 
				methods for identifying and summarizing the properties of encrypted communications (tls/ssl) without the need to open or decrypt the contents of the packets

				in essence, these methods create a digital signature (fingerprint) of the initial handshake of the client with the server

				1. what is ja3 fingerprinting?
					the ja3 method was introduced by the salesforce research team in 2017
					this method only looks at the first packet sent by the client (tls client hello)

					how is ja3 constructed?
						ja3 extracts five distinct parts of the client hello message and concatenates them with a comma (,)
							1. tls version (e.g. 771 for tls 1.2)
							2. cipher suites list (the cipher suites accepted by the client)
							3. extensions list (requested extensions)
							4. elliptic curves list (supported elliptic curve groups)
							5. elliptic curve formats list (curve formats)

						example raw string (ja3 string)
							plaintext
								771,4865-4866-4867-49195-49199,0-23-65281-10-11,29-23-24,0

							this string is then md5-hashed to produce a 32-character code (e7d705a3286e415879ed80005f0aa29b)
							this is the ja3 hash

							each software (chrome, firefox, psiphon, python, different os kernels) sends these 5 parts in a different order and with different values

							so the ja3 hash indicates the exact type of application that generated the traffic

				2. why was ja4 introduced? (what is ja4+?)
					with the introduction of tls 1.3 and new techniques for applications to bypass detection (such as tls grease which puts random values in the client hello), the ja3 model was challenged because small random changes would cause the ja3 hash to change (false positives would increase)

					in 2023, the same team introduced a newer and much more advanced standard called ja4+

					ja4 code structure
						the ja4 output consists of three parts separated by a hyphen (-) (t13d151600_8daaf6152771_03fe0828775f)
							part 1 (readable)
								general specifications such as protocol (tcp/quic), tls version, number of ciphers and extensions

							part 2
								cipher suite sorted hash

							part 3
								extensions sorted hash and alpn

				differences and advantages of ja4 over ja3
					ja3 
						32-character md5 hash
						resistance to tls 1.3 weak (due to grease and random changes)
						coverage scope only tls client (client-side applications)

					ja4
						human-readable code + hash
						resistance to tls 1.3 very high (filters and sorts random values and grease)
						coverage scope  ja4+ suite (includes client, server, http, ssh,...)

				ja3 and ja4 usage in firewalls and security appliances (fortigate)
					 systems (siem, ids/ips, fortigate, cloudflare) use these fingerprints for the following purposes
					 	detecting malware and proxy/vpn tools
					 		malware or programs like psiphon, even if they encrypt their traffic and put the names of reputable sites (google) in sni, their ja3/ja4 fingerprint is exposed
					 		because their handshake structure is different from a real chrome or firefox browser

					 	botnet detection
					 		malicious bots usually use simple libraries (python requests or go) that have a different ja3 than a human browser

					 	threat hunting
					 		finding systems on the internal network that have unusual behaviors at the ssl/tls layer without the need to decrypt traffic

			inspecting, filtering, or allowing traffic from filtering bypass protocols like psiphon is a traditional challenge in firewall and dpi
				the main reason for this challenge is that tools like psiphon are designed to be stealthy and evade ssl inspection

				psiphon is a multi-hop, multi-protocol tool
					if https fails, it will resort to ssh tunnel, obfuscated ssh, domain fronting, quic, and variable proxy servers around the world

				therefore, ssl inspection is only one piece of the puzzle
				
				to thoroughly control psiphon on fortigate, a multi-layered (defense-in-depth) architecture must be implemented
					1. ssl layer / deep inspection (limit protocol tampering)
						when ssl inspection is enabled, fortigate can inspect psiphon tampering with ssl handshakes (such as ssl handshake)
							detect suspicious snis or domain fronting
								psiphon sometimes sends the name of a valid site in the sni (such as cdn.cloudflare.com) but connects to a different location inside the tunnel

								with deep inspection, fortigate detects a mismatch between the http host header and ssl sni and drops the connection

							block ssh over https
								psiphon obfuscates ssh traffic or its own private tunnels to make it look like tls
								deep inspection decrypts this fake structure and drops it because it does not follow the standard tls/ssl patterns

					2. application control layer (behavior pattern detection)
						your most important tool for blocking or allowing psiphon is application control signatures
						fortigate detects psiphon based on traffic signature, not just port or ip
							plaintext
							config application list "block-vpn-list"
							config entries
							edit 1
							set application 15873 <-- psiphon signature code on fortiguard
							set action block <-- or pass to allow
							next
							end
							end

						block pattern
							in the application list, set the proxy category or specifically the psiphon application to block

							also block complementary applications such as ssh.proxy, tor, ultrasurf and quic (psiphon also uses quic over udp 443)

					3. web filtering / isdb layer (psiphon infrastructure blocking)
						psiphon regularly connects to the server list discovery to find active servers

						uses the internet service database (isdb)
							fortigate has a complete database of ips for known proxies and vpns
							in policy you can block the source or destination of these ips

						block web filter categories
							proxy avoidance
							dynamic dns
							malicious websites / anonymizer

					4. control unnecessary ports and protocols (hardening)
						if psiphon sees that port 443 is blocked by deep inspection, it will try its luck on other ports

						block quic (udp 443/80 protocol)
							psiphon is very fond of udp on ports 443 and 53
							if you block udp 443 in the firewall, psiphon will be forced to come to tcp 443, where deep inspection and application control are lurking

						restrict non-standard ports
							limit regular users internet output to only necessary ports (such as 80, 443, 53) and block ports above 1024 or direct ssh outbound (port 22)

				how to contain psiphon on fortigate?
					forcing through inspection layer
						we close udp 443 (quic) and irrelevant ports to force psiphon to pass tcp 443 (https)

					packet opening (deep ssl inspection)
						with deep inspection, we expose fake https tunnels and sni mismatches

					signature recognition (application control)
						with psiphon's unique signature in the control application, we block or allow the packet

		direction 
			checking whether security profiles are bidirectional security profiles in stateful firewalls are not limited to a single path and are evaluated based on the data type

			[ client ] ---- (1) request / outbound (web filter, application control, dns) ----> [ fortigate ]

			[ client ] <--- (2) response / inbound (antivirus, dlp, file filter) -------------- [ fortigate ]
			
			outbound path profiles (request / outbound) these profiles are applied to the client's outgoing request packet
				web filter
					block url or website category before the request leaves

				dns filter
					evaluate and block unauthorized dns queries

				application control
					identify the application in the initial request packets

				waf / ips (some signatures)
					detect attacks such as sqli or xss within the request body

			response / inbound profiles these profiles are applied to data received from the server to the client
				antivirus (av)
					scans incoming files and content to detect malware

				file filter
					prevents receiving files with prohibited extensions (.exe, .bat) 

				dlp (data loss prevention)
					scans incoming content to prevent sensitive information from leaking 

			bidirectional profiles 
				ips
					simultaneous analysis of attacks in requests and exploits in responses

				ssl inspection
					complete management of the outgoing handshake process and decryption/inspection in return

		must used forticare and fortiguard license subscription

		inspection mode
			flow
				patterns deploy on traffics , if see these patterns on flow , drop packets
				speedy check and  forward faster on normal packet size if wanna checked  huge file size  could not inspect cuase some huge files might have encryption and might be malware so in this mode could not  check and inspect

				so must used proxy mode to extract and deep scan files

			proxy
				used asic to scan more files and fast 
				consider resources

			*usually default options on firewalls are  flow mode

			direct filter approach (dfa) matching
				used for pattern matchin with asic cp8 and cp9
				used on flow base (has no store state in fortios)
				packet check dfa or patterns as ultra fast checking mechanism
				if see anomaly drop last file section or partition from client download or usuage flow so clients could not used the files (rst flag)

			on proxy inspection we have no file trasnfer just inspect on fortigate , if were okay send to clients (scan on local storage then send files to clients)
			
			content disarm checked the contents and will analyze all scanning features on files
			mobile malware checked phones and tablets malware
			virus outbreak is feature to scan and detect some network viruses which advertise on devices and nodes (has special signatures)

			sandbox
				used licensed and recieved signature from fortigaurd and forticare

				post transfer scanning	
					first used signature db on local fortigate devicem then send to sandbox cloud
					might advertise infection
					will be on flow and proxy base scanning

				inline scanning
					just used in proxy base
					before checked  local database of signatures on fortigate it generate the hash and send to sandbox cloud
					after these  steps decide what to do

			emulator means inside the fortios we have isolated space to scan some files and check behavioral (used protocol option size file , better used lower values cause  local ssd or storage will be used on this feature)(zeroday attacks will be controled)
			infection quarantine used more space (faz , fmg or more ssd  on local device) for checkc infected files over virtual forlders
			treat exe as virus check the treat on mapi pop3 smtp and imap
			heuristic used ai and machine learning with behavioral detetct and scanning which help us to provide and recommended security configs

			on flow base scan  with ips engine behave like on-the-fly  so more packets with lite resource consumption could be used
			we have no buffer on flow mode

			scan mode
				on old versions
					default
						streaming on small file size
						used wad deamon (ips , ... used this process)
						decompression will be happen

					legacy
						used buffer and checked content (used large size)
						has no chunked fetching , used scanunit deamon

				on new versions
					flow
						no buffer and no scan
						used dfa or on-the-fly scanning with ips and ai

					proxy
						behave like old versions scanning mode and buffering
						has scanunit deamon

				 **the av engine al malware detection model integrates into regular av scanning to help detect potentially malicious windows portable executables (pes) in order to mitigate zero-day attacks
				 	previously, this type of detection was handled by heuristics that analyzed file behavior
				 	with av engine al, the module is trained by fortiguard av against many malware samples to identify file features that make up the malware
				 	
				 	**the av engine al package can be downloaded by fortios via fortiguard on devices with an active av subscription

					simple av configurations to quickly scan a file without handing it off to another process
					the following, more complex feature sets cannot be processed by in-process scan

				stream base scanning
					stream-based scan supports the following archive file types: zip, gzip, bzip2, tar, and iso (iso 9660)
					
					in fortios 7.0, stream-based scan is supported in http(s), ftp(s), and scp/sftp
					in fortios 6.4 and 6.2, stream-based scan is only supported in http(s)
					
					stream-based scan does not support http post
					
					stream-based scan is not supported when the following features are enabled
						dlp
						quarantine
						fortiguard outbreak prevention and external block list
						content disarm

					**if a file is not supported, it is buffered and sent to scanunit for scanning

					 are performed with no oversize limits on a best effort basis
						can inspect the contents of large archive files without buffering the entire file
						decompress and scan the entire archive
						
				**an oversized archive file is a compressed file that is oversized according to the following setting

					end
					
				**if the file is not oversized, it is buffered and sent to scanunit for scanning

			tcp windowing
				when packets start forwarding to destination all segments could be forward more than before
				first step send 128 k then 256 then ...
				if one packets droped all segment size and forwarding will be on half
				tcp  size could be  2g on some applications like winscp
				so effect on main fortigate memeory
					
				**can set stream-base scan with flow control limit as oversize limit

				better do these as static setup not dynamic mode

				config firewall profile-protocol-options
				edit proto-test
				config ssh
				set stream-based-uncompressed-limit 1000
				#the maximum stream-based uncompressed data size that will be scanned, in mb (default = 0 (unlimited))
				#stream-based uncompression used only under certain conditions
				
				set tcp-window-type static
					#system use the system default tcp window size for this protocol (default)
					#static manually specify the tcp window size
					#dynamic vary the tcp window size based on available memory within the limits configured in tcp-window-minimum and tcp-window-maximum
					#auto-tuning used dynamic values on memory

					**better used dynamic and auto-tuning

				set tcp-window-size 1024
				#tcp static window size (65536 - 33554432, default = 262144)

				set tcp-window-minimum 512
				#minimum tcp dynamic window size (65536 - 1048576, default = 131072)

				set tcp-window-maximum 1500
				#maximum tcp dynamic window size (1048576 - 33554432, default =8388608)

				end

				order of sending
					hold until victim  (mainly used on inline scanning)

						fortigate send same file to fortisandbox

						if sandbox sense anomaly  block immediately and clients connection get reset

					normal transfer
						send files to client 
						after execute fortigate send same file to sandbox
						after client use file , fortisanbox check behavioral analytics

		web filter
			web content filter: blocks web pages containing words or patterns that you specify
			
			url filter: uses urls and url patterns to block or exempt web pages from specific sources, or block malicious  urls discovered by fortisandbox
			
			fortiguard web filtering service: provides many additional categories you can use to filter web traffic

			**web filter contains web content filter with weighted words and inspect traffics , url filters used fortisandbox and fortiguard updates with manual definition values

			web filters are applied in the following order
				url filter
				fortiguard web filtering
				web content filter
				web script filter
				antivirus scanning

			on firewall policies must set proxy mode inspection , web filter profile proxy base , inspection mode deep or custom inspection

			url filter with regex block
				on web filter pannel
					url filter
						regex - block - status enable
						.*ya*

			actions
				exempt
					the traffic is allowed to bypass the remaining fortiguard web filters, web content filters, web script filters, antivirus scanning, and dlp proxy operations

				block
					the fortigate denies or blocks attempts to access any url that matches the url pattern. a replacement message is displayed

				allow
					the traffic is passed to the remaining fortiguard web filters, web content filters, web script filters, antivirus proxy operations, and dlp proxy operations
					if the url does not appear in the url list, the traffic is permitted

				monitor
					the traffic is processed the same way as the allow action
					for the monitor action, a log message is generated each time a matching traffic pattern is established

			fortigaurd filter
				analyze too many pages
				fortinet teams check all web content
				web rating is a main task on this part

			credential phisihing
				web filter which checks urls and check clients logon names 
					github.com is our url and has its owned domain controller 
					some clients wanna login into the pages so used u1@github.com
					here fortigate checked all domains urls and credentials
					analyze on antiphishing part

					fortigate beahve like proxy  
						on main website section with user and pass box id and domain names check all credentials 
						proxy all recieved requests to main domain if approved them via dc or radius .... accept connections

					web-based url filter actions and fortiguard category-based filtering have higher priority than antiphishing url filter actions and fortiguard filtering
						if a request is blocked by the web-based url filter or fortiguard filter, there is no further antiphishing scanning
						antiphishing scanning only happens after the web-based url filtes and fortiguard filters allow the traffic

						if a submission matches an entry in the url filter table that has an antiphishing action the defined action is taken no further fortiguard category-based rules are applied

						like firewall rules, the url filter table and fortiguard category-based antiphishing rules use a top-down priority the rule that matches first is the one that is used

			quic algorithem check
				quic (quick udp internet connection or access) defined by google
				http1
					http + tcp + ip + mac

				https
					http + tls + tcp + ip + mac

				http2
					http + hpack and stream + tls 1.2  + tcp + ip + mac

				http3
					http + qpack and stream + quick ( tls 1.3 ) + udp + ip + mac

					quic has built-in feature which help us to encrypt data with tls 1.3 on both side (server and clients) , also forward faster
					used udp and behave like tcp inside quic if lost packet

				differences between http2 and quic
					http2 
						has one tcp connection and many http request over this connection 
						behave like multiplexing

					quic
						many udp connection
						many streams on ud connections

				how quic and tls worked
					clint and server on http2 used first tcp connection then tls negotiate and after these send http2 traffic 

					on quic we have same time negotiation on quic and tls connection (worked faster and liter) then forward http3

				quic packet
					quic packet is also known as quic datagram, which contains a quic header and many quic frames(1,2,3n)

				quic haeder:
					the quic header is an important part of quic packet that contain critical information for packet processing, routing, and managing the connection between endpoints

					spin bit:
						the spin bit is a flag in quic headers that indicates network delay and congestion to the sender 
						the main purpose of using spin bit is for performance monitoring and debugging

					key phrase:
						key phrase is used to identify the encryption keys used to encrypt the packet (tls 1.3 setup)

					destination connection id:
						the destination connection id is an identification number included in the quic header it directs
						incoming packets to the appropriate quic connection at the receiver's end

					packet number:
						the packet number is an important component of quic headers, providing the sequence number
						assigned to each packet during a specific connection

				frame:
					it includes the type, stream id, offset, and stream data stream data is distributed across numerous frames, but it may be constructed using the connection id, stream id, and offset, which are used for displaying the data blocks in the proper sequence

				stream:
					a single quic connection can support either unidirectional or bidirectional data flow each quic	connection supports many independent streams, each with its unique stream id

				feature 			quic (http/3) 											tls 13
				**************************************************************************************************************
				purpose   			faster, more reliable internet connections 				secure encryption for data
				**************************************************************************************************************
				works with   		replaces tcp (uses udp) 		 						works over tcp (http/11, http/2)
				**************************************************************************************************************
				speed   			1-rtt (first visit), 0-rtt (repeat visits)  			1-rtt handshake (faster than tls 12)
				**************************************************************************************************************
				encryption   		built-in (uses tls 13)  								adds encryption to tcp connections
				**************************************************************************************************************
				latency fix   		no "head-of-line blocking" (packets independent)  		reduces handshake time but doesn't fix tcp delays
				**************************************************************************************************************
				best for   			video calls, gaming, unstable networks 					securing websites, apis, emails
				**************************************************************************************************************
				adoption   			new (used by youtube, cloudflare)   					widely used (most websites support it)
				**************************************************************************************************************

		video filters
			must used proxy mode inspection
			each youtube channel has vid or video id
			fortigate track this section

			order of check
				when the wad receives a video query from a client, it extracts the video id (vid) and tries to check the category and channel from the local cache

				if there is no match from the local cache, it connects to the fortiguard video rating server to query the video category
				
				if the fortiguard rating fails, it uses the videofilter youtube-key to communicate with the google api server to get its category and channel id
				this is the api query setting and it requires the user's own youtube api key string
				this configuration is optional

				if all steps fail to match the video, the wad calls on the ips engine to match the video id and channel id from the
				application signature database
					here we check with anycast service

		application control
			applications on database will be on some signatures which detect application with ips protocol decoder
			signature and design patterns followed fortinet policies

			mus have license and subscription

			security profiles
				application control
					new profile app-cont-test
						block or monitor ... on each application categories
						override the main actions
							basically filter on signature or severity 

						options
							quic
								allow means check quic on packets  and behave like admin  policy

								block means if see quic on clients traffic and packets block them

			how analyze and detect application on traffic flows?
				ips protocol decoder
					application control sensors specify what action to take with the application traffic
					application control uses ips protocol decoders that can analyze network traffic to detect application traffic, even if the traffic uses non-standard ports or protocols
					application control supports traffic detection using the http protocol (versions 1.0, 1.1, and 2.0)

					if used http3 or quic might have more load on resources

					decoder
						https://wwwfortinetgurucom/2016/11/configure-ips-options/
						https://techsparxcom/energy-system/learn-modbus-dnp3html#google_vignette

						gtp (gprs tunneling protocol) - mobile networks (3g/4g)
							what is gtp?
								used in mobile carrier networks (3g/4g/5g) for data tunneling
								runs on udp/2123 (gtp-c), udp/2152 (gtp-u)
								vulnerable to tunneling attacks, dos, and fraud

							fortigate gtp decoder features
								gtp message validation: detects malformed gtp headers
								tunnel flooding protection: prevents gtp-c/gtp-u flooding attacks
								fraud detection: blocks illegal imsi/imei spoofing
								zero-day gtp exploit protection: covers cve-2021-45462 (gtp dos)

								**imsi > international  mobile subscriber number or identify , define  format of numbers and ...
									mcc > country codes  (usa 310)
									mnc > network code (at&t 410)
									msin > unique subscriber identifier number  ,  cover social numbers in usa

						modbus - scada & industrial systems
							what is modbus?
								a serial (modbus rtu) or tcp (modbus tcp, port 502) protocol for industrial automation
								used in plcs (programmable logic controllers) and factory equipment
	
							fortigate modbus decoder features
								illegal function codes: blocks unauthorized commands (eg, function code 90 for exploits)
								malformed packet detection: prevents buffer overflow attacks
								excessive request flooding: detects denial-of-service (dos) attempts
								write command protection: blocks unauthorized coil/register writes

						dnp3 (distributed network protocol 3) - industrial control systems (ics)
							what is dnp3?
								a protocol used in scada (supervisory control and data acquisition) and industrial control systems (ics)
								commonly found in electric utilities, water treatment plants, and oil/gas pipelines
								operates over tcp (port 20000) or serial connections
	
							fortigate dnp3 decoder features
								anomaly detection: detects malformed dnp3 packets, buffer overflows, and protocol violations
								function code analysis: monitors read/write commands that could manipulate ics devices
								secure authentication (dnp3-sa): detects weak or missing authentication
								fuzzing & exploit detection: blocks attacks like "dnp3 outstation crash" (cve-2012-4684)

			attributes on another command
				protocol
					application protocol filter 

				risk
					risk or impact of allowing traffic from this application to occur (1 - 5; low (1), elevated (2), medium (3), high (4), and critical (5))
					
				vendor
					application vendor filter (0 - 25, or all)

				technology
					application technology filter
						all
						0 (network-protocol)
						1 (browser-based)
						2 (client-server)
						4 (peer-to-peer)

				behavior
					application behavior filter
						all
						2 (botnet)
						3 (evasive)
						5 (excessive bandwidth)
						6 (tunneling)
						9 (cloud)

				popularity
					application popularity filter (1 - 5, from least to most popular)

				action
					pass/block traffic or reset the connection for traffic from this application (default = block)

				ssl-based application detection over decrypted traffic in a sandwich topology
					when a fortigate is sandwiched between ssl encryption and decryption devices, the fortigate can process the decrypted traffic that passes between those devices
					this feature adds support for decrypted traffic in application control in some pre-defined signatures, the signature is pre-marked with the require_ssl_di tag
					the force-inclusion-ssl-di-sigs option under application list allows users to control the inspection of dissected traffic
					when this option is enabled, the ips engine forces the pre-marked ssl-based signatures to be applied to the decrypted traffic of the respective applications

					config application list
					edit app-cont-test
					set force-inclusion-ssl-di-sigs enable
					end

					**all signatures that include the require_ssl_di tag are pre-defined and cannot be customized
					**if wanna run deep inspection , we have decryption mechanism so if another devices onnetwork send clear text data to fortigate , sense these are unormal so with this feature we can  manage forwarding to another devices

				matching multiple parameters on application control signatures
					application control signatures that support parameters (such as scada protocols) can have multiple parameters grouped together and matched at the same time
					multiple application parameter groups can be added to an override traffic will be flagged if it matches at least one parameter group

				2006-2010 when the payment card industry data security standard (pci dss) began requiring organizations that accept payment cards (credit cards) to install either an ids or a web app firewall, many of those organizations purchased an ids/ips

				the ips technology had been more finely tuned and was much better at not blocking harmless traffic, so people began using it in the ips mode
				meanwhile, botnets were proliferating

				2008 on java and extensions with java applets infected with malwares so normal and old version of ids and ips could not detect them 
				so used next generation firewalls
					sandbox  invent as a main tool for these type detection
						after the 2011 breach on rsa, the security company widely known for its two-factor authentication product
						the media referred to the attack as an advanced persistent threat (apt) and later reported that the breach occurred due to a phishing attack that contared a document tainted with malware

						organizations began asking security vendors if they could protect them from a document or executable that has malware embedded in it if the vendor has no signature for that malware
						most security vendors with an ids/ips offering could not
						customers were clamoring for an apt remedy, so vendors decided the best fix would be to add sandboxing and/or emulation capabilities on the ids/ips, but redesigning the hardware would take anywhere from 12 to 18 months

						companies like fireeye and fidelis were growing they wera delivering a device with sandboxing or emulation capability that no other network security vendor had at the time

						the sandbox was a whole new technology category addressing the ability to find zero-day malware, traffic that contained documents or executables via web or email were sent to a breach detection system

						documents and axeeutables would automatically be opened in the sandbox
						when anything was discovered to have been maficious, the sandbox would send an alert to an administretor

						although ids/ips vendors wouid not be able to create that type of feature for
						at least a year, they begen using md5/sha cheoksums of known bad files. each file has a unique checksum

						checksums (also known as hashes or  signatures) are strings of characters made from numbers and letters, which can be used to verify the integrity of files and text messages
						if the checksum that entered the network metched the checksum the vendor had on file, the sandbox would alert the victim's orgenization that melware had just entered the network back then, this was a milestone for protecting networks

						althougn gartner coined the term next-generation firewell in 2003 and predicted they would include ips features and would be offered in 2006, ngfws were not widely adopted until 2013, at that time, they begen including ids/ips functionality, such as using signatures to identify known attacks and looking for anomalies arid protocol deviations in  the packet flow

						major attacks like wannacry in 2017 and the solarwinds breach in 2020 highlighted the need for more advanced solutions

						during this time, most enterprises adopted ngfws by the latter half of the 2010s, and they continued to expand on features like real-time prevention, identity management, and sandboxing

						ethernalblue is a tool for smb v1 infection
							eternalblue is a leaked nsa exploit of the smb protocol in microsoft windows that is used to propagate the malware in affected systems
							eternalblue leverages a technique called pool grooming-which is a type of heap spray attack on kernel memory structure. targeting vulnerable
							windows systems, it injects a shellcode that enables the attacker to use the ip address of the machine to directly communicate with the smb protocol

							further, without user interaction, it runs a script to check for a tool called doublepulsar to verify if it is already installed and running on the system
							doublepulsar is a backdoor implant tool that was  also developed by the nsa

							eternal blue - piggybacking system
								as mentioned above, eternalblue is a piggybacking system and an smb protocol exploit in windows systems
								this is how it all starts with eternalblue exploit	
								eternalblue.exe runs a script on the target windows computer executing the following commands	
									send an smb echo request to the target machine
									setup the environment to exploit the vulnerable system
									complete smb protocol fingeprinting
									attempt the exploit attack
									if successful, check for doublepulsar malware
									ping doublepulsar for an smb reply

							**doublepulsar means one system send virus to 2 systems and spread as tree

							heap spraying 
								technique used to allocate large blocks of memory on the heap and fill them with specific byte sequences
								it involves creating multiple copies of the attacker's code, often referred to as "nop sleds" or "no-operation sleds"
								if an attacker determines that a given structure, allocated with kmalloc(), is used after being freed, they can attempt a heap-spraying attack by forcing the kernel to allocate a large number of objects from the same bucket

								a new heap spraying technique called heap taichi exploits the weakness in memory alignment

						identification and identity management provide robust identification and access control for the distributed workforce by differentiating between legitimate and illegitimate users, applications, and devices. having full visibility into entities inside an organization's environment regardless of location and platform (on-prem, cloud, mobile, endpoints, or lot devices) provides more robust security across all network traffic

						as covered earlier, sandboxing became a popular technique earlier in the decade, and as machine-learning algorithms became more reliable, ngfws became more powerful and moved beyond static rule-based approaches to analyze patterns, detect anomalies, and even predict potential attacks based on learnings from historical data and continued model tupine

			how write signature
				F-SBID( --name "Block. SMTP.VRFY.CMD"; --protocol tcp; --service SMTP; --pattern "vrfy"; --no_case; --context header; )

				f-sibd is fortinet signature block id (must be upper case)
				--pattern means which term and values must be checked as sensor (case sensetive)
				--no_case means no case sensetive
				--contex header used for http host retrieve and checking

				config ips custom
				edit ips-cus-test
				..
				end

				must used different --attack-id to border signature (automatic assignment)(1000-9999)

				--flow from_client (direction of packet flow)

				signature difinition points
					to match patterns using -- pattern, you must enclose the pattern in double quotation marks (") and follow it with a semicolon

					the special characters ("; \| :) must be written as (|22 |, | 3b | or | 3b | , | 5c | or | 5cl, |7c |or |7cl, |3a | or | 3a |)

					although you can use backslash (\) to escape any character except a semicolon (;), we  do not recommend this

					to match patterns using -- pcre, you must enclose the pattern in double quotation marks (") and follow it with a semicolon (;)

					the special characters ("; /) must be written as (\x22, \x3b or\x3b, \x2f or \x2f). regular expressions should conform to the perl compatible regular expression (pcre) standard
						re worked with python
						preg worked with php
						if need heavy analysis and process used pcre

					pcre matching is the least efficient engine matching algorithm, and it can easily cause performance issues that are difficult to identify through testing

					to detect line endings (for example, when scanning http headers), use "| 0a |", not "| 0d 0a|"

					use as long a pattern as possible with a range limit
					searching for short patterns, with a length less than four, is inefficient for the engine's matching algorithm

					if some encoded content is always the same, you can make a signature to match the encoded form
						check packets on decode and encode mode , create a signature for them and if see encrypted values as same before force the action faster than before

					this allows for detection of the encoded content, even though the engine does not support decoding

					do not use the no_case option on a non-alphabetic pattern

					do not use the no_case option on case-sensitive patterns
					
					some programming languages are case-sensitive, like c, perl, php,  parameters passed to these programs should not use no_case

				range modifier notes
					[ ethernet header ][ ip header ][ tcp/udp header ][ payload (data) ][ trailer ]

					modifier 		purpose
					--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
					distance 		how far after a reference point to start matching
					--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
					within 			maximum bytes after the reference point to searchfor a match
					--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
					depth 			maximum bytes from the start of the payload to search
					--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
					offset 			number of bytes from the start to begin matching
					--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
					nocase 			case-insensitive matching
					--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
					isdataat 		checks if data exists at a specified offset
					--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
					pcre 			per1-compatible regular expression (pcre) matching

					
					reference (<refer>) 	points to
					--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
					payload (default 		the start of the packet's data payload (after headers) fortigate, snort searching http post data
					--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
					http_uri 				the http uri (/index.php?id=1) snort 3, fortigate detecting attacks in urls
					--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
					http_header				http headers (user-agent:) snort 3, fortigate blocking malicious user agents
					--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
					http_cookie 			http cookie values snort 3, fortigate session hijacking detection
					--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
					tcp_payload 			raw tcp payload (after tcp headers) fortigate, snort non-http traffic inspection
					--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
					dce_stub_data 			dce/rpc (ms-rpc) payload , snort , detecting smb exploits
					
					**refer worked on payload or data section

					the offset, depth, offset_abs, and depth_abs keywords are deprecated (used for voip)
						offset used from last payload bit to end
						absolete used inside payload to end

					these keywords are still supported in ips engine 3.0 in order to maintain backward compatibility

					the snort/pcre r option is no longer part of our pcre
						**r used for better matchign
							means from last matching section start process
							r= return

					use -- distance 0; instead

					if you do not use a range modifier with pattern or pcre, matching is done from the beginning to the end of the buffer

					if you only use distance or distance_abs with pattern or pcre, matching is done from the location that is relative to the reference specified by <refer> to the end of the buffer

					if you only use within or within_abs with pattern or pcre, matching is done from the beginning of the reference specified by <refer> to the end of the buffer

					do not omit the <refer> value or set <refer> to match when performing a pattern search with range modifiers

					exercise caution when combining distance, within, distance_abs, and within_abs for the same pattern or pcre
					they should be used in pairs of distance/within and distance_abs/ within_abs, and the <refer> values should be the same
						distance used last matched point toward last bit
						within pointed to new inside section on payload

						**with distance and within combination we could not have best performance so we have more false positive

					r option or other
						modifier 	meaning buffer/inspection	target
						--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
						b 			normalized (decoded) uri	matches the raw uri before normalization (,%20 remains as-is, not converted to space)
						--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
						u 			non-normalized (raw) uri	matches the uri after decoding (%20 becomes a space)
						--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
						p 			post payload 				inspects the body of http post requests
						--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------	
						h 			http header	 				matches against http headers (host:, user-agent:)
						--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
						m 			http method					checks the http request method (get, post)
						--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
						c 			http cookie					searches within http cookies
						--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
						i 			http response body			inspects the body of http responses (server replies)
						--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
						d 			dnp3 data					matches against dnp3 (industrial control system protocol) payloads 
						--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
						k 			smtp email body 			searches the body of smtp (email) messages
						--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
						s 			sticky buffer				keeps a persistent match reference for later rules
						--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
						y 			ssi (server-side includes)	matches against server-side include directives in web content

					http://manual-snort-org.s3-website-us-east-1.amazonaws.com/node32.html#section00451100000000000000
					https://assets.tryhackme.com/cheatsheets/snort%20cheatsheet%20-%20tryhackme.pdf
					https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/legacy/sp/nistspecialpublication800-31.pdf

					typical signature definition errors
						searching for an encoded pattern in a uri
							multiple encoding methods can be used in a uri. in order to have better coverage, you should try to match the decoded pattern whenever possible

							use -- pattern "/ .. / .. /"; -- context uri; rather than -- pattern "/ .. %c0%af .. /"; -- context uri ;

						using special characters that are not escaped in pcre
							this can cause issues with detection. since some characters have special meanings, they should be escaped by a backslash "1", or be expressed in hexadecimal format, like using \x2e for"."

						using the port number instead of -- service
							the ips engine identifies common services. use these service types instead of their port number to define signatures see supported service types on page 8 for the available service types

						using byte_test or byte_jump to perform relative matching after a previous uri match
							since the http decoder has three buffers for uris, relative matching can cause performance issues

						not specifying the best context value, or not including any context
							using patterns to match the uri, header, banner, host, or body sectors of http, imap, smtp, pop3 or ssh traffic without using the -- context keyword, reduces efficiency and increases the possibility of false positives

							set signature 'F-SBID( -- name "block-iran-fqdn"; -- pattern "iran. ir"; -- service SSL; -- no_case; -- flow from_client; -- context host;)'

							set signature "F-SBID( -- attack_id 1468; -- name \"block-iran-gov-fqdn\"; -- pattern \"iran.gov.ir\"; -- service SSL; -- no_case; -- flow from_client; -- context host;)"

							**--service means our isnpect starts on which protocol parameters and values with what attributes

			custom signature 2020
				must set name , service, protocol and flow on signature on fortigate as custom signature
				if wanna used  fortimanager  better set attack id
					version
						6 to 6.0.6
						6.2.0 to 6.2.2

				name
					mandatory
						the string should be enclosed by double quotation marks
						the maximum length of a signature name is 64 characters
						the period replaces the use of a space
						the signature name must be unique for each custom signature

					company.name.productname.vulnerablefile.vulnerabilityname

					--name "IBM.Domino.iNotes.Foldername.Buffer.Overflow";

				service
					must used upper case terms on services

					you can only use the service keyword once in a signature

					the fortinet ips engine marks traffic based on packet content instead of port mapping
					you can use the service keyword to scan traffic not running on a standard service port

					if a packet is marked by a protocol dissector as some type of service, for http, it will only be inspected by
					signatures in the http service tree

					consequently, if a signature uses -- dst_port 80 instead of -- service http, it will not be matched
					you must ensure that all signatures detecting traffic with an implemented service  type have the service keyword

					if a signature has the service keyword, it will be added to the corresponding service tree
					a signature's service tree assignment determines which packets it will scan
					ips has multiple service trees (http, smtp, pop3, dns,..) and one unknown service tree

					if a signature has no service keyword, but it has a port keyword, it will be added into the unknown service tree
					
					custom signatures where the service type is unknown_service are added to all service trees
					
					if a signature has neither a service keyword nor a port keyword, it is a generic signature, and will be added to all service trees including the unknown_service tree

				flow
					from client
						matches packets sent from the client to the server

					from server
						matches packets sent from the server to the client

					bi direction
						matches packets sent from the client to the server and from the server to the client

					reversed
						specifies that the attack is in the opposite direction from the detected packet
						a typical caseis when a brute force login is detected by matching a server packet indicating that a login has failed
						this keyword will not affect detection
						its purpose is to tell the gui to display the correct location for the vulnerability (client or server)

					--flow bi_direction; dst_port 123;
					#match if source or destination port is 123

				ip header option
					ip-id
						used for spoofed or hidden ip address
						nmap detection

				special characters
					dedicated values or equals used =
					not equal != or not!
					& mixed values
					| or
					^ xor (just check modified bit)
					ranges on [] used in term

				tcp headers
					seq
						on each  tcp packet transmission we have a different value
						if wanna check session hijacking
							if on time base transmission we saw a unusuall increasment could detect the  session hijacking

							--seq <number>[,relative];

							equal to:--seq =,<number>[,relative];
							
							greater than:--seq >,<number>[,relative]:;
							
							less than:--seq <,<number>[,relative];
							
							not equal to :--seq !,<number>[,relative];
							
							the optional field relative indicates the value is relative to the initial sequence number of the tcp session
							no prefix defaults to "equal to."

							--seq <,12345;
							--seq !,12345;

					*usually used relative section as hijacking part

				icmp
					icmp_id
						Check for the specified ICMP ID value. This keyword is only used for packets with ICMP type ECHO_REQUEST or ECHO REPLY
					
						--icmp_id <number>;

					icmp_seq
						Check for the specified ICMP sequence value. This keyword is only used for packets with ICMP type ECHO_REQUEST or ECHO REPLY

						--icmp_seq <number>;

				payload options
					you can use these options to detect contents in the payload of a packet or stream. ips signatures use pattern matching for inspecting a packet payload
					a pattern definition starts with a -- pattern or a -- pcre option name, and is followed by a series of modifiers

					-- pattern <string>; [ -- context c;] [ -- no_case; ] [ -- distance n[,<refer>]]; [ -- within n [,<refer>]];

					or, for pcre patterns
						-- pcre <string>; [ -- context c;] [ -- distance n[,<refer>]]; [ -- within n [,<refer>]];

					**the ips engine handles pcre a lot slower compared to normal pattern matching
						pcre should be used very carefully, especially for signatures that detect traffic from http servers or traffic that does not specify a port

							.*/
							/.+
							\a
							\e

					modifier 	effect
					i 			case-insensitive match
					S 			lets . match newlines 
					m 			multi-line mode: ^/$ match per line 
					X 			ignore whitespace (for readability) 
					a 			force match at start of buffer (like A) -- pcre "/\ahttp\/1\.1/a" (strict http version check) 
					e 			$ matches only end of buffer 
					g 			non-greedy by default (inverts ? behavior) rarely used; prefer explicit .*?


					example use case
					--pcre "/admin/i" (matches admin, Admin)
					--pcre "/<script> .*?< \/script>/s" (matches multiline js)
					--pcre "/^login=/m" (matches login= at start of any line)
					--pcre "/user\s *= \s*[^;]+/x"
					--pcre "/\.exe\e/" (matches .exe only at payload end)

				context
					check the packets and data content

					uri , origin ,....

					file
						decoded attachments for email protocols
						data sessions for ftp
						the body for http

					the ips engine supports "packet-based" inspection, which means it inspects packets even if there are no sessions associated with them many keywords
						for matching tcp/ip header fields, are enabled in packet-based inspection

						if a pattern has the context value packet, packet origin, or no context, it will be inspected using packet-based inspection

					the banner and body are in the packet buffer
					there is no body context in ftp, so file context should be used instead

					for http, the body context and the file context are the same. you can use either -- context file or --context body to indicate where to match the pattern

					mime parsing is supported for the email protocols smtp, imap, pop3, and nntp
					we decode most of the encoding methods, including base64, uuencode, 7/8bit

					quota, binary, and quoted-printable
					
					if the file itself is zipped or archived, the engine currently does not decompress it

					the content, uri, header, and body keywords are deprecated
					they are still supported in ips engine 3.0 (and above) in order to maintain backward compatibility, but don't use them when creating new signatures

					the u, h, and b modifiers have been removed from pcre use context to specify these conditions for pcre matching
						u
							match the decoded uri buffers
							uri
						
						h
							match normalized httpheader
							header
						
						b
							don't use the decoded buffers
							packet_origin

					mime parsing is supported for the email protocols smtp, imap, pop3 and nntp
					currently, all attachments fall under -- context file
					most of the encoding methods are decoded, including base64, uuencode, 7/8bit, quota, binary, and quoted-printable

					for email protocols, some signatures used to use -- context body for base64 encoded strings in attachments
					they will not work anymore, because base64 is now decoded and the strings are located in the file context

					for email protocols, use -- context body to inspect content located in the body and is not an attachment 

					there is no body context in ftp, so the file context should be used. for example, a signature like this should be able to match the eicar file in all of these protocols as it does not specify a service context and it uses file context
						f-sbid( -- protocol tcp; -- pattern "x50!p"; -- context file; )

					if the file is zipped or archived, the engine currently does not decompress it

				distance, distance_abs, within, within_abs
					use these four keywords to specify the range (in bytes) of where the engine will search for a pattern
						distance indicates the offset from the last reference point to start searching for a pattern
						within indicates the range of bytes from the last reference point which the engine should search for a pattern
	
					-- distance <range> [,<refer>];
					-- distance abs <range>[,<refer>];
					-- within <range> [, <refer>];
					-- within abs <range>[, <refer>];
	
					the <refer> field is the reference point for the <range>
					if it is not included, the default is match

					match 		the reference is the last matched pattern. this is the default setting
					packet 		the reference is the beginning of the packet
					context 	the reference is the beginning of the pattern context
					reverse 	search for the pattern relative to the end of the packet or context. this is only accepted with the --distance option, and the reference must be packet or context
					lasttag 	the reference is the one set by last pset

					--pattern "login"
					--distance 10 --within 20 
					--pattern "fail"
						from byte 0 till login term we have 10 byte gap 

						0---------------10
						-----------login
						|   distance	|----(20byte within)------|
												must match fail term here

					Search for the pattern within 50 bytes of the last matched pattern:
						-- pattern "/disp_album.php?"; -- context uri; -- no_case; -- within 50,context;
						-- pattern "|05 00|"; -- distance 0; -- pattern "|6e 00|"; -- distance 5; -- within 2;

					Count 10 bytes back from the end of the packet, then search for the pattern within 5 bytes:
						-- pattern "Host: "; -- context header; -- pattern !"|0a|"; -- context header; within abs 80; -- distance 10,packet, reverse; -- within 5, packet;

				important for custom ips
					if you use the keywords distance and within with the first pattern of a signature, set the <refer> field to context, as there are no previous matched patterns

					the keywords distance and distance_abs indicate the minimum distance from the end of the last reference point to the beginning of the current pattern

					the distance is counted from the next character after the last reference point
					both these keywords support negative range value
					in this case, distance does not require the designated amount of data before the reference point while distance_abs does

					the following signature makes sure no ? character is before the / bbbb pattern in the uri:
						-- pattern "/bbbb"; -- context uri; -- within 200, context; -- pattern!"?"; -- context uri; --distance 200; -- within 200;

					this signature works even if the /bbbb pattern in the uri is not preceded by 200 bytes of data

					the keywords within and within_abs require that the whole pattern appear within the given range following the last reference point

					if the distance or distance_abs keywords are also present, with the same reference point, the pattern will be matched from the specified distance to the range of bytes specified by the within or within_abs keywords

					use the keywords distance abs and within_abs only for negative matches (patterns with the ! modifier)
					they indicate that the buffer following the reference point must be longer than or equal to the value specified by <range>
						--pattern !"|0a|"; -- within 100, match;
						--pattern !"|0a|"; -- within_abs 100, match;

						**abs means  you must have free space on buffer and memory to scan an analyze data

					if the buffer after the previous match is shorter than 100, the first signature is matched
					it is not recommended to use distance_abs and within_abs for a positive match because the behavior of these keywords is unreliable
					it is better to use the keyword data_at instead
						--pattern "bbbbbb"; -- pattern "dddddd"; -- within_abs 200;
						--pattern "bbbbbb"; -- data_at 200, relative; -- pattern "dddddd";

					these two signatures are equivalent but the second one is recommended for a reliable match (cause relative check buffer and ram at the first steps then process)
					a negative <range> value can be used to specify the range before the reference
					different types and references can be combined as range modifiers

				byte_jump, byte_test
					use the byte_jump keyword to move the reference point
					the distance to be skipped is calculated from the value of bytes at a specified offset

					use the byte_test keyword to compare the value of bytes at the specified offset with a given value
					the keyword does not move the reference point

					if the data to be processed or skipped is beyond the end of the packet, the option is considered unmatched.
						-- byte_jump &lt ;* |bytes&gt;,&lt;offset&gt; [, &lt;multiplier&gt; [, &lt;modifiers&gt; ] ] ;
						--byte_test&lt;*bytes&gt;,&lt;op&gt;,&lt;value&gt;,&lt;offset&gt;[,&lt;multiplier&gt; [,&lt;modifiers&gt;]];

					2 > 100 means check 2 byte of data if were greater than 100 alert me
					
					**check tcp value payload size

					-- byte_jump 4,0, relative;
					-- byte_test 4,>, 3536, 0, relative;
					-- byte_jump 4,20, relative, align;
					-- byte_jump 4,0,4, relative, little;
					-- byte_test 4,>, 0x7FFF, 4, relative;
					-- byte_ttest 4,>, $PKT_SIZE, 4, relative;
					-- byte_test 4,>, $PKT SIZE, 4, 2, relative;

					big and little endian
						when designed  computer language , a format created for  string of data and values  presentation
							xyz aa bb cc dd xyz

							how  read and write values

							big endian used 
								dd cc bb aa

							little endian (last and most significant)
								msb (most) aa
								lsb (last) dd

							**usually used big endian
								cause navigate  on all data and converting standards must used unique format

					align
						if data were 7 bit make them rounded to 8 or ...
						rounds the number of converted bytes up to the next 32bit boundary, only used with byte_jump

						sparc algorithem (scalable processor architect ,  how process and distribute data on cpu)
							byte 0-7

							half 0-15

							word 0-31

							double 0-63

				parsed type
					--parsed_type HTTP-GET
						if we have many get values on application we can set which get we needed to inspect 

						F-SBID( -name "SSL. PCT.Overflow"; -- protocol tcp; -- dst port 443; -- flow from client; --tag test,!Tag.SSLv3.Web.443; -- tag test, !Tag.SSLv2.Web.443; -- tag test, !Tag. TLSv1. Web. 443; -- pattern "|01|"; -- within 1, packet; -- distance 2, packet; -- byte_test 2,>,1,3; -- byte_test 2,<, 0x301, 3; -- byte_test 2,>, 0,5; -- byte_test 2,!,0,7; -- byte_test 2,<,16,7; -- byte_test  2,>, 16, 9; -- byte_test 2,<, 33, 9; -- pattern "|8F|"; -- within 1,packet; -- distance 11, packet; -- byte_test 2,>, 32768, 0, relative; -- data size >300; )

						F-SBID( -- name "SSL.PCT.Overflow"; -- protocol tcp; -- flow from client; -- service SSL; --parsed_type SSL_PCT ;--pattern "|01|"; -- within 1, packet; -- distance 2, packet; -- byte_test 2,>,1,3; -- byte_test 2,<, 0x301, 3; -- byte_test 2,>, 0,5; -- byte_test 2, !, 0,7; -- byte_test 2,<,16, 7; -- byte_test 2,>,16,9; -- byte_test 2,<,33, 9; -- pattern "|8F|"; -- within 1,packet; -- distance 11,packet; -- byte_test 2,>, 32768, 0, relative; -- data_size >300; )

				rate and track
					if fill ranges and values faster than usuall means we have unormal request

					--rate <count>,<duration>[,<limit>];

					forti used  average all values
						time and requests

						**limit say  behave strictly and  if reach dedicated values , bock them

					how track users
						src_ip
						dst ip
						dhcp_client
						dns domain
						dns domain and ip

						if -- track is specified, only matched packets which have the same specified keyword tracked are added to the counter

						if -- rate is used without -- track, all matched packets are added to the counter and the signature is reported once the threshold is reached

						ips counts the average number of packets over a period of time. this might allow some extra packets to go through
						therefore, to ensure accuracy, the 1imit keyword was added to allow counting to be done in a strict time
						when limit is enabled the packet count is more accurate

				tag
					use this keyword in a signature to mark a session with a named tag, or to check whether a tag has been set for a session
	
					pattern matching with ips signatures is essentially packet-base
					the tag keyword is mainly used when attack patterns appear in more than one packet or in different directions
					a signature that matches an earlier packet in an attack can mark the session with a named tag, and the existence of the tag can be tested when ensuing packets in the same session are scanned
	
					the matching algorithm guarantees the order in which signatures are scanned
					the signatures are sorted based on their tag dependencies
					during packet inspection, the signatures are matched in this order, so that signatures that depend on other signatures are always scanned later in the process	
					
					--tag <op>, [!}<name>;

						[ ! ] is only allowed in test operations
						it returns true if the tag does not exist
	
						the <op> value determines which operation is performed

						pset
							mark the session with a named tag and remember the last reference point
							this reference point can be referred by using lasttag for keywordsdistance, within, distance_abs, andwithin_abs

						toggle
							toggle the specified tag (set <=>clear) in the session

						test
							test the existence of the specified tag. add ! if the signature is to test the nonexistence of the specified tag

						-- tag set, Tag. Rsync.Argument;
						-- tag clear, tag.login;
						-- tag test, Tag. Rsync. Argument;
						-- tag test, !DHTML.EDIT.CONTROL.CLSID;

						the name of a tag should only contain printable characters. it should not contain spaces, commas, exclamation marks, or semicolons
						by default, a newly-created tag is in the un-set state
						patterns in tag set and tag test signatures can appear in the same packet together

					weight
						use this keyword to specify the weight to be assigned to the signature. while optional, this keyword is useful because 	it allows a signature with the higher weight to have priority over a signature with a lower weight

						the weight must be between 0 and 255
						most of the signatures in the application control signature database have weights of 10; botnet signatures are set to 250

						range of 20 to 50 is recommended for custom signatures
		
						-- weight <weight_int>;

	
						f-sbid( -- attack_id 8151; -- vuln_id 8151; -- name "windows. nt. 5.web. surfing"; -- default action drop_session; -- service http; -- protocol tcp; -- app_cat 25; -- flow from_client; --pattern !"fct"; -- pattern "windows nt 5.1"; -- no_case; -- context header; -- weight 40; )
